Right around the time they brought in this new regional manager. Young guy, maybe thirty-something, slick hair, always wearing those suits that look a little too tight. He started coming down on me, you know? Taking away accounts I'd handled for years. People I knew! Like Jim over at the auto parts distributor. I knew Jim's kids' names, for crying out loud. I went to his daughter's wedding! Beautiful ceremony, by the way, out by the lake. They had this incredible prime rib at the reception. But this new manager, he just swoops in and hands Jim's account over to some kid fresh out of college who probably doesn't even know what a carburetor is. It's just... it's not the way you do business. You build trust, you know? But yeah, about eight months of this... this emptiness.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, not really. I mean, I used to. I used to love bowling on Thursday nights. We had this league, the Pin Pals. Corny, I know, but we had a blast. My buddy Frank was on the team, and Frank... well, Frank had this hook shot that would just defy physics. He swore it was because he used a special rosin bag, but I think he just had weird wrists. He was a plumber, you know, so maybe he just had strong hands from turning wrenches all day. Plumbers make good money now, too. I should have learned a trade.\n\nBut anyway... yeah, bowling. And I used to like working out in the garden. Tomatoes, mostly. Beefsteaks. There's nothing like a fresh tomato right off the vine, still warm from the sun, with just a little salt. You buy them in the store now and they taste like wet cardboard. They pick 'em green and gas 'em, you know. It's a shame. \n\nAnd my wife, she loves those little art gallery shows downtown. We used to go, and maybe go dancing after. We used to do all that. But now... I don't know, doc. I just sit there. When she asks if I want to go, I just make excuses. Tell her my back hurts or I'm too tired. It just... none of it feels worth the effort anymore. Even the tomatoes. I just let the weeds take over this year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess that's exactly it. Loss of interest. Like I was saying about the bowling, and the garden... taking my wife out to those gallery shows. I used to really look forward to all of that. Especially the dancing. We used to go to this place, The Starlight Room. They had a live band on weekends. A real brass section, not like the synthesized stuff you hear on the radio now. The trumpet player, a guy named Sal, he could really wail. He actually played with some big names back in the seventies, or so he claimed. He had this vintage horn, beautiful silver thing... polished it between every set. \n\nBut yeah, the interest is just... gone. Like I said, I just sit there now. Make excuses. None of it feels worth the effort anymore. Even the dancing. Even the tomatoes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Ending it? Well... I've been having these thoughts, doc. Thoughts that maybe my wife and kids would just be better off if I went to sleep and didn't wake up. It's a terrible thing to admit out loud. \n\nI mean, I do have a good life insurance policy for them. I bought it from this guy Phil back in '95. Phil was a real character, always wore these loud Hawaiian shirts, even in November. He claimed he had a thyroid thing that kept him running hot, but I think he just liked the attention, you know? He actually moved down to Boca a few years back. Sent me a postcard with a giant pink flamingo on it just last Christmas. Nice guy, terrible golfer though. He had this wicked slice that would always end up in the rough. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at least with the policy they'd be okay financially if something happened. But having these thoughts... they really scare me. They scare me enough that I just don't trust myself the way I used to. I never used to be a guy who thought about things like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Yeah, my sleep is just... it's a mess, doc. At night I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, replaying everything from work over and over in my head. I've been a sales rep for, what, almost thirty years now? Started out selling office supplies, back when people still used fax machines and carbon paper. Carbon paper! Can you imagine? You'd get that blue ink all over your hands, it was a nightmare to wash off. My buddy Dave used to joke that we looked like Smurfs by the end of the week. Dave was a funny guy, always had a joke. He actually retired last year, bought an RV and is driving around the country with his wife. Good for him, you know? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the sleep. When I finally do manage to drift off, I always wake up around three or four in the morning. Wide awake. And I'm already just... worn out. Exhausted before the day even begins, just dreading having to get up and do it all over again. As for my appetite... I don't know, I just haven't really been paying attention to food much lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? I don't... I don't really know if I am. I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, maybe my wife has, you'd have to ask her. She's always noticing things I miss. Like last week, she pointed out this crack in the driveway that I swear wasn't there before. The guy who poured that concrete, Vinnie, he swore it would last fifty years. He was a real character, Vinnie. Always wore this gold chain with a little boxing glove charm on it. Said his uncle was a professional fighter back in the sixties, fought at Madison Square Garden once. I don't know if I believe him, but he sure liked to talk about it. He had this old beat-up Ford truck that sounded like a tractor when he started it up. \n\nBut anyway... no, I haven't really noticed moving or talking any slower myself. I just feel so tired all the time, you know? But I don't think I'm actually moving in slow motion or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking slower? I don't... I don't really think so. I haven't noticed my voice changing or anything like that. \n\nYou know who did talk slow, though? My old regional manager, Gary. Man, getting a full sentence out of Gary was like watching paint dry. He'd just pause between every other word. He was from somewhere down in Georgia originally, had this real thick drawl that he never lost, even after twenty years up here. He used to bring in these homemade pecan pies his wife made for the holidays. Best pie I ever had, honestly. She used this dark syrup, I don't know what it was, but it made the pecans stick together almost like peanut brittle. I asked him for the recipe once, but he said it was a family secret. They ended up retiring to Florida a few years back. Seems like everybody is moving down to Florida these days. The humidity down there, though... I went to Orlando for a sales conference back in '08 and I swear I sweat through two dress shirts before noon. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, me talking slow. No, I don't think I am. I just... I don't have as much to say these days, I guess. I'm just so drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss or gain over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? I... I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, a long time. The battery in our bathroom scale died right around Thanksgiving, and I just never bothered to get a new one. It's one of those digital ones, you know? My wife bought it at Bed Bath & Beyond right before they shut all their stores down. It's really a shame, how many of those big stores are just gone now. You drive down Route 9 and half the strip malls are just empty storefronts. \n\nThere used to be this amazing little Italian deli in one of them, Sal's. Sal made the best meatball sub you've ever had. He'd toast the bread just right so it wouldn't get soggy from the marinara. I used to stop there for lunch all the time back when I was covering the north territory. He had this huge signed poster of Frank Sinatra right behind the register, and he always had the oldies playing on this little radio by the meat slicer. I wonder whatever happened to Sal when that plaza got bought out. Probably retired, I guess. \n\nBut, um, wait. You asked about my weight. Yeah, my clothes don't really feel any looser or tighter, I guess. So probably no real change. I just haven't cared enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sitting still more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: Resting more? I... I don't know, maybe. I spend a lot of time in the recliner these days. It's this big brown leather one we got from Raymour & Flanigan a few years ago. We got it on their Memorial Day sale, actually. The salesman there, this young kid named Tyler, he tried so hard to upsell us on the matching sofa, but I knew what he was doing. I've been in sales my whole life, you can't pull that on me. He had a good pitch, though, I'll give him that. Reminded me of when I first started out, selling copier service contracts door-to-door in the industrial parks. You had to have thick skin for that. People would practically slam the door in your face before you even got your name out. I remember this one guy, owned a printing shop, he actually chased me out with a rolled-up newspaper. Can you imagine? A newspaper. You don't even see those much anymore. Everybody just reads everything on their phones now. My nephew, he's always staring at that little screen, even at the dinner table. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, right, sitting still. Yeah, I guess I do sit in that recliner a lot now. I just... I don't have the energy to get up and do much else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're fidgeting or restless more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I'm mostly just sitting in that chair I mentioned. I don't really have the nervous energy for fidgeting, you know? \n\nMy brother-in-law, Dave, now he's a fidgeter. The guy cannot sit still to save his life. Always bouncing his knee, clicking a pen, tapping his fingers. He's an accountant, works over at that big firm downtown. You'd think a numbers guy would be calm, right? Just sitting at a desk all day. But no, he's a bundle of nerves. He's always complaining about the traffic on the commute in. I tell him, Dave, you gotta listen to audiobooks or something, calm yourself down. I used to listen to those old Zig Ziglar tapes on cassette back when I had my first company car. A '92 Taurus. Great car, terrible transmission, though. I had to get it rebuilt twice before it hit a hundred thousand miles. The mechanic I went to, this guy Stan, he said Ford just didn't build them right that year. Stan was a character. He had this old golden retriever that just slept in the corner of the garage all day, covered in grease. \n\nWait, what was the question? Restless? No, no. I'm just... I'm too tired to be restless. Just heavy, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: Pacing? No, I... I don't really pace. I don't feel like I need to be moving around at all. Actually, it's funny you mention pacing, because my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he used to pace his driveway every single morning. Six AM, rain or shine, there he was, walking back and forth from the garage to the mailbox. He said it was for his heart, but I think he just wanted to get away from his wife for a bit. She was a real talker, Mrs. Henderson. Nice lady, but she'd corner you at the fence and tell you all about her daughter's problems down in Florida. The daughter moved to Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Clearwater. Somewhere on the Gulf side. I took the wife down to Sarasota once for an anniversary trip. Beautiful beaches, that white powdery sand that doesn't get hot on your feet. We stayed at this little motel right on the water, run by this couple from Montreal. They made the best crepes for breakfast. I haven't had a good crepe since then. \n\nBut, um, constant motion? No. Like I said before, I'm just mostly sitting in my chair. Moving just takes too much effort lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your loved ones?\n\nPatient: A burden? Yeah... Guilty? Yeah, I guess guilt is a big part of it. It goes back to what I was just saying, really. I just keep telling myself I'm useless now. I look back at all those years of taking care of clients, providing for the family, making sure everyone had what they needed... and it feels like none of it even counts anymore. Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller ran the place until he was like 85. He always had a jar of those root beer barrel candies on the counter. I loved those things as a kid. They don't make candy like they used to, you know? Everything is all artificial flavors and high fructose corn syrup now. I was watching this documentary on TV the other night about how much sugar is in everything. It's in bread, it's in pasta sauce... you can't escape it. 

But anyway, yeah, I just... I feel so guilty because I can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I just feel useless.

But yeah, about eight months of this... this emptiness.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, not really. I mean, I used to. I used to love bowling on Thursday nights. We had this league, the Pin Pals. Corny, I know, but we had a blast. My buddy Frank was on the team, and Frank... well, Frank had this hook shot that would just defy physics. He swore it was because he used a special rosin bag, but I think he just had weird wrists. He was a plumber, you know, so maybe he just had strong hands from turning wrenches all day. Plumbers make good money now, too. I should have learned a trade.\n\nBut anyway... yeah, bowling. And I used to like working out in the garden. Tomatoes, mostly. Beefsteaks. There's nothing like a fresh tomato right off the vine, still warm from the sun, with just a little salt. You buy them in the store now and they taste like wet cardboard. They pick 'em green and gas 'em, you know. It's a shame. \n\nAnd my wife, she loves those little art gallery shows downtown. We used to go, and maybe go dancing after. We used to do all that. But now... I don't know, doc. I just sit there. When she asks if I want to go, I just make excuses. Tell her my back hurts or I'm too tired. It just... none of it feels worth the effort anymore. Even the tomatoes. I just let the weeds take over this year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess that's exactly it. Loss of interest. Like I was saying about the bowling, and the garden... taking my wife out to those gallery shows. I used to really look forward to all of that. Especially the dancing. We used to go to this place, The Starlight Room. They had a live band on weekends. A real brass section, not like the synthesized stuff you hear on the radio now. The trumpet player, a guy named Sal, he could really wail. He actually played with some big names back in the seventies, or so he claimed. He had this vintage horn, beautiful silver thing... polished it between every set. \n\nBut yeah, the interest is just... gone. Like I said, I just sit there now. Make excuses. None of it feels worth the effort anymore. Even the dancing. Even the tomatoes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Ending it? Well... I've been having these thoughts, doc. Thoughts that maybe my wife and kids would just be better off if I went to sleep and didn't wake up. It's a terrible thing to admit out loud. \n\nI mean, I do have a good life insurance policy for them. I bought it from this guy Phil back in '95. Phil was a real character, always wore these loud Hawaiian shirts, even in November. He claimed he had a thyroid thing that kept him running hot, but I think he just liked the attention, you know? He actually moved down to Boca a few years back. Sent me a postcard with a giant pink flamingo on it just last Christmas. Nice guy, terrible golfer though. He had this wicked slice that would always end up in the rough. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at least with the policy they'd be okay financially if something happened. But having these thoughts... they really scare me. They scare me enough that I just don't trust myself the way I used to. I never used to be a guy who thought about things like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Yeah, my sleep is just... it's a mess, doc. At night I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, replaying everything from work over and over in my head. I've been a sales rep for, what, almost thirty years now? Started out selling office supplies, back when people still used fax machines and carbon paper. Carbon paper! Can you imagine? You'd get that blue ink all over your hands, it was a nightmare to wash off. My buddy Dave used to joke that we looked like Smurfs by the end of the week. Dave was a funny guy, always had a joke. He actually retired last year, bought an RV and is driving around the country with his wife. Good for him, you know? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the sleep. When I finally do manage to drift off, I always wake up around three or four in the morning. Wide awake. And I'm already just... worn out. Exhausted before the day even begins, just dreading having to get up and do it all over again. As for my appetite... I don't know, I just haven't really been paying attention to food much lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? I don't... I don't really know if I am. I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, maybe my wife has, you'd have to ask her. She's always noticing things I miss. Like last week, she pointed out this crack in the driveway that I swear wasn't there before. The guy who poured that concrete, Vinnie, he swore it would last fifty years. He was a real character, Vinnie. Always wore this gold chain with a little boxing glove charm on it. Said his uncle was a professional fighter back in the sixties, fought at Madison Square Garden once. I don't know if I believe him, but he sure liked to talk about it. He had this old beat-up Ford truck that sounded like a tractor when he started it up. \n\nBut anyway... no, I haven't really noticed moving or talking any slower myself. I just feel so tired all the time, you know? But I don't think I'm actually moving in slow motion or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking slower? I don't... I don't really think so. I haven't noticed my voice changing or anything like that. \n\nYou know who did talk slow, though? My old regional manager, Gary. Man, getting a full sentence out of Gary was like watching paint dry. He'd just pause between every other word. He was from somewhere down in Georgia originally, had this real thick drawl that he never lost, even after twenty years up here. He used to bring in these homemade pecan pies his wife made for the holidays. Best pie I ever had, honestly. She used this dark syrup, I don't know what it was, but it made the pecans stick together almost like peanut brittle. I asked him for the recipe once, but he said it was a family secret. They ended up retiring to Florida a few years back. Seems like everybody is moving down to Florida these days. The humidity down there, though... I went to Orlando for a sales conference back in '08 and I swear I sweat through two dress shirts before noon. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, me talking slow. No, I don't think I am. I just... I don't have as much to say these days, I guess. I'm just so drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss or gain over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? I... I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, a long time. The battery in our bathroom scale died right around Thanksgiving, and I just never bothered to get a new one. It's one of those digital ones, you know? My wife bought it at Bed Bath & Beyond right before they shut all their stores down. It's really a shame, how many of those big stores are just gone now. You drive down Route 9 and half the strip malls are just empty storefronts. \n\nThere used to be this amazing little Italian deli in one of them, Sal's. Sal made the best meatball sub you've ever had. He'd toast the bread just right so it wouldn't get soggy from the marinara. I used to stop there for lunch all the time back when I was covering the north territory. He had this huge signed poster of Frank Sinatra right behind the register, and he always had the oldies playing on this little radio by the meat slicer. I wonder whatever happened to Sal when that plaza got bought out. Probably retired, I guess. \n\nBut, um, wait. You asked about my weight. Yeah, my clothes don't really feel any looser or tighter, I guess. So probably no real change. I just haven't cared enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sitting still more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: Resting more? I... I don't know, maybe. I spend a lot of time in the recliner these days. It's this big brown leather one we got from Raymour & Flanigan a few years ago. We got it on their Memorial Day sale, actually. The salesman there, this young kid named Tyler, he tried so hard to upsell us on the matching sofa, but I knew what he was doing. I've been in sales my whole life, you can't pull that on me. He had a good pitch, though, I'll give him that. Reminded me of when I first started out, selling copier service contracts door-to-door in the industrial parks. You had to have thick skin for that. People would practically slam the door in your face before you even got your name out. I remember this one guy, owned a printing shop, he actually chased me out with a rolled-up newspaper. Can you imagine? A newspaper. You don't even see those much anymore. Everybody just reads everything on their phones now. My nephew, he's always staring at that little screen, even at the dinner table. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, right, sitting still. Yeah, I guess I do sit in that recliner a lot now. I just... I don't have the energy to get up and do much else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're fidgeting or restless more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I'm mostly just sitting in that chair I mentioned. I don't really have the nervous energy for fidgeting, you know? \n\nMy brother-in-law, Dave, now he's a fidgeter. The guy cannot sit still to save his life. Always bouncing his knee, clicking a pen, tapping his fingers. He's an accountant, works over at that big firm downtown. You'd think a numbers guy would be calm, right? Just sitting at a desk all day. But no, he's a bundle of nerves. He's always complaining about the traffic on the commute in. I tell him, Dave, you gotta listen to audiobooks or something, calm yourself down. I used to listen to those old Zig Ziglar tapes on cassette back when I had my first company car. A '92 Taurus. Great car, terrible transmission, though. I had to get it rebuilt twice before it hit a hundred thousand miles. The mechanic I went to, this guy Stan, he said Ford just didn't build them right that year. Stan was a character. He had this old golden retriever that just slept in the corner of the garage all day, covered in grease. \n\nWait, what was the question? Restless? No, no. I'm just... I'm too tired to be restless. Just heavy, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: Pacing? No, I... I don't really pace. I don't feel like I need to be moving around at all. Actually, it's funny you mention pacing, because my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he used to pace his driveway every single morning. Six AM, rain or shine, there he was, walking back and forth from the garage to the mailbox. He said it was for his heart, but I think he just wanted to get away from his wife for a bit. She was a real talker, Mrs. Henderson. Nice lady, but she'd corner you at the fence and tell you all about her daughter's problems down in Florida. The daughter moved to Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Clearwater. Somewhere on the Gulf side. I took the wife down to Sarasota once for an anniversary trip. Beautiful beaches, that white powdery sand that doesn't get hot on your feet. We stayed at this little motel right on the water, run by this couple from Montreal. They made the best crepes for breakfast. I haven't had a good crepe since then. \n\nBut, um, constant motion? No. Like I said before, I'm just mostly sitting in my chair. Moving just takes too much effort lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your loved ones?\n\nPatient: A burden? Yeah... I guess I do. I just keep telling myself I’m useless now. I used to be the guy everyone relied on, you know? I took care of my family, took care of my clients. I had this one client, managing a big logistics firm out in Jersey. Paul was his name. He wouldn't sign a service contract with anyone but me. We used to go out to this steakhouse after closing a deal, the kind of place where they bring the plates out sizzling hot with the butter popping. You gotta be careful not to touch the plate, the waiters always warn you, but my buddy Gary touched it once and got a blister the size of a dime on his thumb. Gary was a good guy. He ended up moving out to Arizona for his asthma. Supposed to be a dry heat out there, better for the lungs. I always wanted to take the family out to see the Grand Canyon, maybe rent one of those big RVs, but we just never found the time with the kids' sports schedules and all the travel I was doing for work... \n\nWhere was I going with this? Oh, right. Being a burden. It just... it feels like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family, it just doesn't even count anymore. None of it matters, because I can’t even handle this job anymore. I can't handle much of anything. I'm just useless to them now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah, I guess guilt is a big part of it. It goes back to what I was just saying, really. I just keep telling myself I'm useless now. I look back at all those years of taking care of clients, providing for the family, making sure everyone had what they needed... and it feels like none of it even counts anymore. Just wiped out, because I can't even handle this job now. \n\nIt's like that time we had a massive snowstorm back in '96. You remember that one? The Blizzard of '96 they called it. Dumped like three feet of snow over the weekend. I spent six hours shoveling the driveway just so my wife could get her car out on Monday morning. I had this old aluminum shovel, the kind with the wooden handle that gives you splinters if you don't wear gloves. I bought it at this hardware store that used to be over on 4th Street. Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller ran the place until he was like 85. He always had a jar of those root beer barrel candies on the counter. I loved those things as a kid. They don't make candy like they used to, you know? Everything is all artificial flavors and high fructose corn syrup now. I was watching this documentary on TV the other night about how much sugar is in everything. It's in bread, it's in pasta sauce... you can't escape it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just... I feel so guilty because I can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I just feel useless.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Every day? Yeah. Yeah, I'd say so. It's every single day now. I used to look forward to things, you know? Like bowling. I was in a league on Thursday nights down at the lanes on Elm. They had these great chili cheese dogs at the snack bar. Not exactly healthy, I know, but you need a little something after throwing a heavy ball for two hours. My ball was a custom drill, a really nice Brunswick. I bought it off this guy who was retiring and moving down to Boca. Everyone moves to Florida eventually, it seems like. 

But now... I don't even take the ball out of the closet. Same with the garden. I used to love working out in the yard, getting my hands dirty. I grew tomatoes one year that were the size of softballs. Big Boy tomatoes, they were called. You slice one of those up with a little salt and pepper, maybe some mozzarella... best sandwich you'll ever eat. My wife used to make her own pesto with the basil I grew. She's a great cook. 

I used to love taking her out, too. We'd go to gallery shows downtown, or go dancing on the weekends. There was this club that played big band music on Saturday nights. We'd get dressed up... I had this sharp navy suit I'd wear. 

But now? Now I just sit there in my chair. She asks if I want to go out, and I just make excuses. It just... none of it feels worth the effort anymore. It's just easier to stay put. Every day is like that. Just sitting there.

You'd get that blue ink all over your hands, it was a nightmare to wash off. My buddy Dave used to joke that we looked like Smurfs by the end of the week. Dave was a funny guy, always had a joke. He used to bring in these homemade pecan pies his wife made for the holidays. Best pie I ever had, honestly. She was a real talker, Mrs. Henderson. Nice lady, but she'd corner you at the fence and tell you all about her daughter's problems down in Florida. The daughter moved to Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Clearwater. Somewhere on the Gulf side. I took the wife down to Sarasota once for an anniversary trip. Beautiful beaches, that white powdery sand that doesn't get hot on your feet. I guess I do. I just keep telling myself I’m useless now. I used to be the guy everyone relied on, you know? I took care of my family, took care of my clients. I had this one client, managing a big logistics firm out in Jersey. Paul was his name. He wouldn't sign a service contract with anyone but me. We used to go out to this steakhouse after closing a deal, the kind of place where they bring the plates out sizzling hot with the butter popping. You gotta be careful not to touch the plate, the waiters always warn you, but my buddy Gary touched it once and got a blister the size of a dime on his thumb. Gary was a good guy. He ended up moving out to Arizona for his asthma. Supposed to be a dry heat out there, better for the lungs. I always wanted to take the family out to see the Grand Canyon, maybe rent one of those big RVs, but we just never found the time with the kids' sports schedules and all the travel I was doing for work... \n\nWhere was I going with this? None of it matters, because I can’t even handle this job anymore. I can't handle much of anything. I'm just useless to them now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah, I guess guilt is a big part of it. It goes back to what I was just saying, really. I just keep telling myself I'm useless now. I look back at all those years of taking care of clients, providing for the family, making sure everyone had what they needed... and it feels like none of it even counts anymore. Just wiped out, because I can't even handle this job now. \n\nIt's like that time we had a massive snowstorm back in '96. You remember that one? The Blizzard of '96 they called it. Dumped like three feet of snow over the weekend. I spent six hours shoveling the driveway just so my wife could get her car out on Monday morning. I had this old aluminum shovel, the kind with the wooden handle that gives you splinters if you don't wear gloves. I bought it at this hardware store that used to be over on 4th Street. Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller ran the place until he was like 85. He always had a jar of those root beer barrel candies on the counter. I loved those things as a kid. They don't make candy like they used to, you know? Everything is all artificial flavors and high fructose corn syrup now. I was watching this documentary on TV the other night about how much sugar is in everything. It's in bread, it's in pasta sauce... you can't escape it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just... I feel so guilty because I can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I just feel useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your lack of interest in activities is present every day?\n\nPatient: Every day? I spend a lot of time in the recliner these days. It's this big brown leather one we got from Raymour & Flanigan a few years ago. We got it on their Memorial Day sale, actually. The salesman there, this young kid named Tyler, he tried so hard to upsell us on the matching sofa, but I knew what he was doing. I've been in sales my whole life, you can't pull that on me. He had a good pitch, though, I'll give him that. Reminded me of when I first started out, selling copier service contracts door-to-door in the industrial parks. You had to have thick skin for that. People would practically slam the door in your face before you even got your name out. I remember this one guy, owned a printing shop, he actually chased me out with a rolled-up newspaper. Can you imagine? A newspaper. You don't even see those much anymore. Everybody just reads everything on their phones now. My nephew, he's always staring at that little screen, even at the dinner table. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, right, sitting still. Yeah, I guess I do sit in that recliner a lot now. I just... I don't have the energy to get up and do much else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're fidgeting or restless more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I'm mostly just sitting in that chair I mentioned. I don't really have the nervous energy for fidgeting, you know? \n\nMy brother-in-law, Dave, now he's a fidgeter. The guy cannot sit still to save his life. Always bouncing his knee, clicking a pen, tapping his fingers. He's an accountant, works over at that big firm downtown. You'd think a numbers guy would be calm, right? Just sitting at a desk all day. But no, he's a bundle of nerves. He's always complaining about the traffic on the commute in. I tell him, Dave, you gotta listen to audiobooks or something, calm yourself down. I used to listen to those old Zig Ziglar tapes on cassette back when I had my first company car. A '92 Taurus. Great car, terrible transmission, though. I had to get it rebuilt twice before it hit a hundred thousand miles. The mechanic I went to, this guy Stan, he said Ford just didn't build them right that year. Stan was a character. He had this old golden retriever that just slept in the corner of the garage all day, covered in grease. \n\nWait, what was the question? Restless? No, no. I'm just... I'm too tired to be restless. Just heavy, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: Pacing? No, I... I don't really pace. I don't feel like I need to be moving around at all. Actually, it's funny you mention pacing, because my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he used to pace his driveway every single morning. Six AM, rain or shine, there he was, walking back and forth from the garage to the mailbox. He said it was for his heart, but I think he just wanted to get away from his wife for a bit. She was a real talker, Mrs. Henderson. Nice lady, but she'd corner you at the fence and tell you all about her daughter's problems down in Florida. The daughter moved to Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Clearwater. Somewhere on the Gulf side. I took the wife down to Sarasota once for an anniversary trip. Beautiful beaches, that white powdery sand that doesn't get hot on your feet. We stayed at this little motel right on the water, run by this couple from Montreal. They made the best crepes for breakfast. I haven't had a good crepe since then. \n\nBut, um, constant motion? No. Like I said before, I'm just mostly sitting in my chair. Moving just takes too much effort lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your loved ones?\n\nPatient: A burden? Yeah... I guess I do. I just keep telling myself I’m useless now. I used to be the guy everyone relied on, you know? I took care of my family, took care of my clients. I had this one client, managing a big logistics firm out in Jersey. Paul was his name. He wouldn't sign a service contract with anyone but me. We used to go out to this steakhouse after closing a deal, the kind of place where they bring the plates out sizzling hot with the butter popping. You gotta be careful not to touch the plate, the waiters always warn you, but my buddy Gary touched it once and got a blister the size of a dime on his thumb. Gary was a good guy. He ended up moving out to Arizona for his asthma. Supposed to be a dry heat out there, better for the lungs. I always wanted to take the family out to see the Grand Canyon, maybe rent one of those big RVs, but we just never found the time with the kids' sports schedules and all the travel I was doing for work... \n\nWhere was I going with this? Oh, right. Being a burden. It just... it feels like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family, it just doesn't even count anymore. None of it matters, because I can’t even handle this job anymore. I can't handle much of anything. I'm just useless to them now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah, I guess guilt is a big part of it. It goes back to what I was just saying, really. I just keep telling myself I'm useless now. I look back at all those years of taking care of clients, providing for the family, making sure everyone had what they needed... and it feels like none of it even counts anymore. Just wiped out, because I can't even handle this job now. \n\nIt's like that time we had a massive snowstorm back in '96. You remember that one? The Blizzard of '96 they called it. Dumped like three feet of snow over the weekend. I spent six hours shoveling the driveway just so my wife could get her car out on Monday morning. I had this old aluminum shovel, the kind with the wooden handle that gives you splinters if you don't wear gloves. I bought it at this hardware store that used to be over on 4th Street. Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller ran the place until he was like 85. He always had a jar of those root beer barrel candies on the counter. I loved those things as a kid. They don't make candy like they used to, you know? Everything is all artificial flavors and high fructose corn syrup now. I was watching this documentary on TV the other night about how much sugar is in everything. It's in bread, it's in pasta sauce... you can't escape it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just... I feel so guilty because I can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I just feel useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your lack of interest in activities is present every day?\n\nPatient: Every day? Yeah. Yeah, I'd say so. It's every single day now. I used to look forward to things, you know? Like bowling. I was in a league on Thursday nights down at the lanes on Elm. They had these great chili cheese dogs at the snack bar. Not exactly healthy, I know, but you need a little something after throwing a heavy ball for two hours. My ball was a custom drill, a really nice Brunswick. I bought it off this guy who was retiring and moving down to Boca. Everyone moves to Florida eventually, it seems like. \n\nBut now... I don't even take the ball out of the closet. Same with the garden. I used to love working out in the yard, getting my hands dirty. I grew tomatoes one year that were the size of softballs. Big Boy tomatoes, they were called. You slice one of those up with a little salt and pepper, maybe some mozzarella... best sandwich you'll ever eat. My wife used to make her own pesto with the basil I grew. She's a great cook. \n\nI used to love taking her out, too. We'd go to gallery shows downtown, or go dancing on the weekends. There was this club that played big band music on Saturday nights. We'd get dressed up... I had this sharp navy suit I'd wear. \n\nBut now? Now I just sit there in my chair. She asks if I want to go out, and I just make excuses. It just... none of it feels worth the effort anymore. It's just easier to stay put. Every day is like that. Just sitting there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Worse at a particular time? I don't know... I haven't really noticed it being worse in the morning or the evening. It's just kind of always there. Mornings used to be my favorite time of day, actually. I'd be up at 5:30 sharp, hit the road before the traffic got bad on the interstate. I had this whole route planned out for my sales calls. There was this diner, Al's Diner, right off exit 14. I'd stop there every Tuesday on my way to see a client down in Trenton. Al made the best scrambled eggs. He always used a little bit of heavy cream in them, said that was the secret to keeping them fluffy. My wife tried doing that once, but she used half-and-half and it just wasn't the same. Half-and-half is too watery, you know? You really need the thick stuff. We used to buy the glass bottles of milk from this local dairy when the kids were little... they'd deliver it right to the porch in this insulated metal box. You don't see milkmen anymore. It's a shame. Everything is just plastic jugs at the big box supermarkets now, and it seems like it expires so fast... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, the time of day. Yeah, no, I don't think it matters what time it is on the clock now. Morning, night... it's all just the same. I don't really notice a difference.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc... I came in because... honestly, most days I just wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling right here in my chest. It's like... it's like the old Ruben is just gone. The guy who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day. \n\nI've been in sales for, gosh, almost thirty years now. Service contracts, mostly. Back in the day, it was all face-to-face, you know? You'd take a client out to lunch, maybe grab a steak at that place downtown—O'Malley's. They closed down a few years back, turned into one of those fancy coffee shops where you can't even get a regular cup of joe without learning Italian. My wife loved their onion rings, though... O'Malley's, I mean, not the coffee shop. \n\nBut... anyway, what was I saying? Yeah, I just... I don't feel like that guy anymore. Just that hollow feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this hollow feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on for... gosh, about eight months now. Right around the time they brought in this new regional manager. Young guy, maybe thirty-something, slick hair, always wearing those suits that look a little too tight. He started coming down on me, you know? Taking away accounts I'd handled for years. People I knew! Like Jim over at the auto parts distributor. I knew Jim's kids' names, for crying out loud. I went to his daughter's wedding! Beautiful ceremony, by the way, out by the lake. They had this incredible prime rib at the reception. But this new manager, he just swoops in and hands Jim's account over to some kid fresh out of college who probably doesn't even know what a carburetor is. It's just... it's not the way you do business. You build trust, you know? But yeah, about eight months of this... this emptiness.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, not really. I mean, I used to. I used to love bowling on Thursday nights. We had this league, the Pin Pals. Corny, I know, but we had a blast. My buddy Frank was on the team, and Frank... well, Frank had this hook shot that would just defy physics. He swore it was because he used a special rosin bag, but I think he just had weird wrists. He was a plumber, you know, so maybe he just had strong hands from turning wrenches all day. Plumbers make good money now, too. I should have learned a trade.\n\nBut anyway... yeah, bowling. And I used to like working out in the garden. Tomatoes, mostly. Beefsteaks. There's nothing like a fresh tomato right off the vine, still warm from the sun, with just a little salt. You buy them in the store now and they taste like wet cardboard. They pick 'em green and gas 'em, you know. It's a shame. \n\nAnd my wife, she loves those little art gallery shows downtown. We used to go, and maybe go dancing after. We used to do all that. But now... I don't know, doc. I just sit there. When she asks if I want to go, I just make excuses. Tell her my back hurts or I'm too tired. It just... none of it feels worth the effort anymore. Even the tomatoes. I just let the weeds take over this year.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess that's exactly it. Loss of interest. Like I was saying about the bowling, and the garden... taking my wife out to those gallery shows. I used to really look forward to all of that. Especially the dancing. We used to go to this place, The Starlight Room. They had a live band on weekends. A real brass section, not like the synthesized stuff you hear on the radio now. The trumpet player, a guy named Sal, he could really wail. He actually played with some big names back in the seventies, or so he claimed. He had this vintage horn, beautiful silver thing... polished it between every set. \n\nBut yeah, the interest is just... gone. Like I said, I just sit there now. Make excuses. None of it feels worth the effort anymore. Even the dancing. Even the tomatoes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Ending it? Well... I've been having these thoughts, doc. Thoughts that maybe my wife and kids would just be better off if I went to sleep and didn't wake up. It's a terrible thing to admit out loud. \n\nI mean, I do have a good life insurance policy for them. I bought it from this guy Phil back in '95. Phil was a real character, always wore these loud Hawaiian shirts, even in November. He claimed he had a thyroid thing that kept him running hot, but I think he just liked the attention, you know? He actually moved down to Boca a few years back. Sent me a postcard with a giant pink flamingo on it just last Christmas. Nice guy, terrible golfer though. He had this wicked slice that would always end up in the rough. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at least with the policy they'd be okay financially if something happened. But having these thoughts... they really scare me. They scare me enough that I just don't trust myself the way I used to. I never used to be a guy who thought about things like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Yeah, my sleep is just... it's a mess, doc. At night I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, replaying everything from work over and over in my head. I've been a sales rep for, what, almost thirty years now? Started out selling office supplies, back when people still used fax machines and carbon paper. Carbon paper! Can you imagine? You'd get that blue ink all over your hands, it was a nightmare to wash off. My buddy Dave used to joke that we looked like Smurfs by the end of the week. Dave was a funny guy, always had a joke. He actually retired last year, bought an RV and is driving around the country with his wife. Good for him, you know? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the sleep. When I finally do manage to drift off, I always wake up around three or four in the morning. Wide awake. And I'm already just... worn out. Exhausted before the day even begins, just dreading having to get up and do it all over again. As for my appetite... I don't know, I just haven't really been paying attention to food much lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? I don't... I don't really know if I am. I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, maybe my wife has, you'd have to ask her. She's always noticing things I miss. Like last week, she pointed out this crack in the driveway that I swear wasn't there before. The guy who poured that concrete, Vinnie, he swore it would last fifty years. He was a real character, Vinnie. Always wore this gold chain with a little boxing glove charm on it. Said his uncle was a professional fighter back in the sixties, fought at Madison Square Garden once. I don't know if I believe him, but he sure liked to talk about it. He had this old beat-up Ford truck that sounded like a tractor when he started it up. \n\nBut anyway... no, I haven't really noticed moving or talking any slower myself. I just feel so tired all the time, you know? But I don't think I'm actually moving in slow motion or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking slower? I don't... I don't really think so. I haven't noticed my voice changing or anything like that. \n\nYou know who did talk slow, though? My old regional manager, Gary. Man, getting a full sentence out of Gary was like watching paint dry. He'd just pause between every other word. He was from somewhere down in Georgia originally, had this real thick drawl that he never lost, even after twenty years up here. He used to bring in these homemade pecan pies his wife made for the holidays. Best pie I ever had, honestly. She used this dark syrup, I don't know what it was, but it made the pecans stick together almost like peanut brittle. I asked him for the recipe once, but he said it was a family secret. They ended up retiring to Florida a few years back. Seems like everybody is moving down to Florida these days. The humidity down there, though... I went to Orlando for a sales conference back in '08 and I swear I sweat through two dress shirts before noon. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, me talking slow. No, I don't think I am. I just... I don't have as much to say these days, I guess. I'm just so drained.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss or gain over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? I... I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, a long time. The battery in our bathroom scale died right around Thanksgiving, and I just never bothered to get a new one. It's one of those digital ones, you know? My wife bought it at Bed Bath & Beyond right before they shut all their stores down. It's really a shame, how many of those big stores are just gone now. You drive down Route 9 and half the strip malls are just empty storefronts. \n\nThere used to be this amazing little Italian deli in one of them, Sal's. Sal made the best meatball sub you've ever had. He'd toast the bread just right so it wouldn't get soggy from the marinara. I used to stop there for lunch all the time back when I was covering the north territory. He had this huge signed poster of Frank Sinatra right behind the register, and he always had the oldies playing on this little radio by the meat slicer. I wonder whatever happened to Sal when that plaza got bought out. Probably retired, I guess. \n\nBut, um, wait. You asked about my weight. Yeah, my clothes don't really feel any looser or tighter, I guess. So probably no real change. I just haven't cared enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sitting still more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: Resting more? I... I don't know, maybe. I spend a lot of time in the recliner these days. It's this big brown leather one we got from Raymour & Flanigan a few years ago. We got it on their Memorial Day sale, actually. The salesman there, this young kid named Tyler, he tried so hard to upsell us on the matching sofa, but I knew what he was doing. I've been in sales my whole life, you can't pull that on me. He had a good pitch, though, I'll give him that. Reminded me of when I first started out, selling copier service contracts door-to-door in the industrial parks. You had to have thick skin for that. People would practically slam the door in your face before you even got your name out. I remember this one guy, owned a printing shop, he actually chased me out with a rolled-up newspaper. Can you imagine? A newspaper. You don't even see those much anymore. Everybody just reads everything on their phones now. My nephew, he's always staring at that little screen, even at the dinner table. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, right, sitting still. Yeah, I guess I do sit in that recliner a lot now. I just... I don't have the energy to get up and do much else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're fidgeting or restless more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I'm mostly just sitting in that chair I mentioned. I don't really have the nervous energy for fidgeting, you know? \n\nMy brother-in-law, Dave, now he's a fidgeter. The guy cannot sit still to save his life. Always bouncing his knee, clicking a pen, tapping his fingers. He's an accountant, works over at that big firm downtown. You'd think a numbers guy would be calm, right? Just sitting at a desk all day. But no, he's a bundle of nerves. He's always complaining about the traffic on the commute in. I tell him, Dave, you gotta listen to audiobooks or something, calm yourself down. I used to listen to those old Zig Ziglar tapes on cassette back when I had my first company car. A '92 Taurus. Great car, terrible transmission, though. I had to get it rebuilt twice before it hit a hundred thousand miles. The mechanic I went to, this guy Stan, he said Ford just didn't build them right that year. Stan was a character. He had this old golden retriever that just slept in the corner of the garage all day, covered in grease. \n\nWait, what was the question? Restless? No, no. I'm just... I'm too tired to be restless. Just heavy, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: Pacing? No, I... I don't really pace. I don't feel like I need to be moving around at all. Actually, it's funny you mention pacing, because my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he used to pace his driveway every single morning. Six AM, rain or shine, there he was, walking back and forth from the garage to the mailbox. He said it was for his heart, but I think he just wanted to get away from his wife for a bit. She was a real talker, Mrs. Henderson. Nice lady, but she'd corner you at the fence and tell you all about her daughter's problems down in Florida. The daughter moved to Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Clearwater. Somewhere on the Gulf side. I took the wife down to Sarasota once for an anniversary trip. Beautiful beaches, that white powdery sand that doesn't get hot on your feet. We stayed at this little motel right on the water, run by this couple from Montreal. They made the best crepes for breakfast. I haven't had a good crepe since then. \n\nBut, um, constant motion? No. Like I said before, I'm just mostly sitting in my chair. Moving just takes too much effort lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your loved ones?\n\nPatient: A burden? Yeah... I guess I do. I just keep telling myself I’m useless now. I used to be the guy everyone relied on, you know? I took care of my family, took care of my clients. I had this one client, managing a big logistics firm out in Jersey. Paul was his name. He wouldn't sign a service contract with anyone but me. We used to go out to this steakhouse after closing a deal, the kind of place where they bring the plates out sizzling hot with the butter popping. You gotta be careful not to touch the plate, the waiters always warn you, but my buddy Gary touched it once and got a blister the size of a dime on his thumb. Gary was a good guy. He ended up moving out to Arizona for his asthma. Supposed to be a dry heat out there, better for the lungs. I always wanted to take the family out to see the Grand Canyon, maybe rent one of those big RVs, but we just never found the time with the kids' sports schedules and all the travel I was doing for work... \n\nWhere was I going with this? Oh, right. Being a burden. It just... it feels like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family, it just doesn't even count anymore. None of it matters, because I can’t even handle this job anymore. I can't handle much of anything. I'm just useless to them now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah, I guess guilt is a big part of it. It goes back to what I was just saying, really. I just keep telling myself I'm useless now. I look back at all those years of taking care of clients, providing for the family, making sure everyone had what they needed... and it feels like none of it even counts anymore. Just wiped out, because I can't even handle this job now. \n\nIt's like that time we had a massive snowstorm back in '96. You remember that one? The Blizzard of '96 they called it. Dumped like three feet of snow over the weekend. I spent six hours shoveling the driveway just so my wife could get her car out on Monday morning. I had this old aluminum shovel, the kind with the wooden handle that gives you splinters if you don't wear gloves. I bought it at this hardware store that used to be over on 4th Street. Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller ran the place until he was like 85. He always had a jar of those root beer barrel candies on the counter. I loved those things as a kid. They don't make candy like they used to, you know? Everything is all artificial flavors and high fructose corn syrup now. I was watching this documentary on TV the other night about how much sugar is in everything. It's in bread, it's in pasta sauce... you can't escape it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just... I feel so guilty because I can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I just feel useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your lack of interest in activities is present every day?\n\nPatient: Every day? Yeah. Yeah, I'd say so. It's every single day now. I used to look forward to things, you know? Like bowling. I was in a league on Thursday nights down at the lanes on Elm. They had these great chili cheese dogs at the snack bar. Not exactly healthy, I know, but you need a little something after throwing a heavy ball for two hours. My ball was a custom drill, a really nice Brunswick. I bought it off this guy who was retiring and moving down to Boca. Everyone moves to Florida eventually, it seems like. \n\nBut now... I don't even take the ball out of the closet. Same with the garden. I used to love working out in the yard, getting my hands dirty. I grew tomatoes one year that were the size of softballs. Big Boy tomatoes, they were called. You slice one of those up with a little salt and pepper, maybe some mozzarella... best sandwich you'll ever eat. My wife used to make her own pesto with the basil I grew. She's a great cook. \n\nI used to love taking her out, too. We'd go to gallery shows downtown, or go dancing on the weekends. There was this club that played big band music on Saturday nights. We'd get dressed up... I had this sharp navy suit I'd wear. \n\nBut now? Now I just sit there in my chair. She asks if I want to go out, and I just make excuses. It just... none of it feels worth the effort anymore. It's just easier to stay put. Every day is like that. Just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your lack of interest in activities is worse at any particular time of day?\n\nPatient: Worse at a particular time? I don't know... I haven't really noticed it being worse in the morning or the evening. It's just kind of always there. Mornings used to be my favorite time of day, actually. I'd be up at 5:30 sharp, hit the road before the traffic got bad on the interstate. I had this whole route planned out for my sales calls. There was this diner, Al's Diner, right off exit 14. I'd stop there every Tuesday on my way to see a client down in Trenton. Al made the best scrambled eggs. He always used a little bit of heavy cream in them, said that was the secret to keeping them fluffy. My wife tried doing that once, but she used half-and-half and it just wasn't the same. Half-and-half is too watery, you know? You really need the thick stuff. We used to buy the glass bottles of milk from this local dairy when the kids were little... they'd deliver it right to the porch in this insulated metal box. You don't see milkmen anymore. It's a shame. Everything is just plastic jugs at the big box supermarkets now, and it seems like it expires so fast... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, the time of day. Yeah, no, I don't think it matters what time it is on the clock now. Morning, night... it's all just the same. I don't really notice a difference.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I had this old aluminum shovel, the kind with the wooden handle that gives you splinters if you don't wear gloves. I bought it at this hardware store that used to be over on 4th Street. Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller ran the place until he was like 85. He always had a jar of those root beer barrel candies on the counter. I loved those things as a kid. They don't make candy like they used to, you know? Everything is all artificial flavors and high fructose corn syrup now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worse than a few months ago? Well, this whole thing has been going on for about eight months now. It all started when the new regional manager came in. Guy's name is Greg. He's one of those corporate types, you know? Wears those slim-fit suits and talks about "synergy" and "optimizing pipelines." He started coming down on me hard, taking away accounts I'd handled for years. Accounts I built from the ground up! Like the Henderson account. I remember when I first signed Henderson, it was back in '04. Or maybe '05? No, '04, because that was the year my daughter got her braces off. We took her to this steakhouse to celebrate, since she could finally eat corn on the cob and chew ice again. She loved chewing ice. My dentist always said that's terrible for your enamel, chewing ice. He's this older guy, Dr. Aris. He has this aquarium in his waiting room with these beautiful angelfish. I always wanted an aquarium, but the maintenance is just... it's a lot of work. You have to balance the pH levels and clean the filters. My neighbor has a koi pond in his backyard, and raccoons keep getting into it. Raccoons are smart, you know. They have those little hands... 

Anyway, yeah, about eight months it's been like this. Ever since Greg started taking my accounts. So I guess it's been pretty bad for a while now.
